=== SOURCE CODE (cpp) ===

#include <bitset>
#include <sstream>
#include <iomanip>

#include <string>

class NumberConverter {
public:
    static std::string decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num);
    static int binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num);
    static int octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num);
    static int hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num);
};

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num) {
    std::string binary_num = std::bitset<32>(decimal_num).to_string();
    binary_num.erase(0, binary_num.find_first_not_of('0')); 
    return binary_num.empty() ? "0" : binary_num;
}

int NumberConverter::binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num) {
    return std::stoi(binary_num, nullptr, 2);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::oct << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num) {
    return std::stoi(octal_num, nullptr, 8);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::hex << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num) {
    return std::stoi(hex_num, nullptr, 16);
}

#include <bitset>
#include <sstream>
#include <iomanip>

#include <string>

class NumberConverter {
public:
    static std::string decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num);
    static int binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num);
    static int octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num);
    static int hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num);
};

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num) {
    std::string binary_num = std::bitset<32>(decimal_num).to_string();
    binary_num.erase(0, binary_num.find_first_not_of('0')); 
    return binary_num.empty() ? "0" : binary_num;
}

int NumberConverter::binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num) {
    return std::stoi(binary_num, nullptr, 2);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::oct << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num) {
    return std::stoi(octal_num, nullptr, 8);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::hex << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num) {
    return std::stoi(hex_num, nullptr, 16);
}

Let's define a helper function `_stoi(s, base)` that returns int or raises ValueError/OverflowError.

Algorithm for _stoi(s, base):
- Input: string s, base (2,8,16)
- We need to parse as C++ std::stoi.
- Steps:
  1. Discard leading whitespace (isspace: space, \t, \n, \v, \f, \r). In Python, str.isspace() is similar but also includes other Unicode whitespace. C++ std::isspace with default C locale? Actually std::stoi uses std::isspace from <cctype> with current C locale? Usually C locale. Python's str.isspace() includes more Unicode. To be identical, we should use ASCII whitespace only: ' ', '\t', '\n', '\v', '\f', '\r'. We'll implement a set.
  2. After whitespace, check for optional sign: '+' or '-'. If present, record sign and move.
  3. Then parse digits valid for base:
     - base 2: '0','1'
     - base 8: '0'-'7'
     - base 16: '0'-'9','a'-'f','A'-'F'
  4. Take as many digits as possible. If no digits after sign (or after whitespace if no sign), throw invalid_argument (ValueError).
  5. Convert the digit string to integer using int(digits, base) (digits are valid, no sign). Apply sign.
  6. Check if result fits in int (32-bit signed). If not, throw out_of_range (OverflowError).
  7. Return result.

We need to define class NumberConverter with static methods.

Let's implement helper `_stoi` as a private static method or module-level function. Since it's only used internally, we can define a nested function or a static method. To keep class structure, we can define a static method `_stoi` inside class.

But the original C++ class only has public static methods. Adding a private helper is fine.

Let's write:

class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def _stoi(s, base):
        # mimic std::stoi(s, nullptr, base)
        i = 0
        n = len(s)
        # skip leading whitespace (ASCII)
        while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
            i += 1
        sign = 1
        if i < n and (s[i] == '+' or s[i] == '-'):
            if s[i] == '-':
                sign = -1
            i += 1
        # parse digits
        if base == 2:
            valid = set('01')
        elif base == 8:
            valid = set('01234567')
        elif base == 16:
            valid = set('0123456789abcdefABCDEF')
        else:
            raise ValueError("unsupported base")
        start = i
        while i < n and s[i] in valid:
            i += 1
        if i == start:
            raise ValueError("invalid literal for int() with base %d: %r" % (base, s))
        digits = s[start:i]
        # Convert digits to integer
        val = int(digits, base)
        val *= sign
        # Check 32-bit signed int range
        if val < -2147483648 or val > 2147483647:
            raise OverflowError("int out of range")
        return val

But wait: std::stoi also accepts "0x" prefix? No, it doesn't. Our parser stops at 'x'. Good.

But what about base 16 digits: 'a' through 'f' and 'A' through 'F'. Good.

Now, note: Python's int(digits, base) for digits like "000" returns 0. Good.

Now, one subtlety: std::stoi for base 2 with string "+-1": after sign '+', next char '-' is not digit, so no digits -> invalid_argument. Our parser: sign consumed, then i at '-', not in valid, so i == start -> ValueError. Good.

Now, another subtlety: std::stoi for base 16 with string "0x10": parse '0', then 'x' invalid, returns 0. Our parser: valid digits include '0', parse '0', stop at 'x', val=0. Good.

Now, what about "  +0x10"? skip whitespace, sign '+', parse '0', stop at 'x', val=0. Good.

Now, what about "  -0x10"? sign '-', parse '0', val=0. Good.

Now, what about "  + 1"? After sign, space is not digit, so no digits -> invalid. Good.

Now, what about empty string after whitespace? No digits -> invalid.

Now, range check: For "2147483648" in base 10? Not relevant. For base 16 "80000000" = 2147483648, > INT_MAX, raise OverflowError. For base 16 "7fffffff" = 2147483647, ok. For base 16 "-80000000" = -2147483648, ok. For base 16 "-80000001" = -2147483649, raise.

Now, is std::stoi's out_of_range thrown for values that are too large in magnitude but the sign is negative? Yes.

Now, what about the case where the digit string is empty but there was a sign? C++ throws invalid_argument. Our code does.

Now, we need to ensure that the exception types are reasonable. In Python, ValueError for invalid_argument, OverflowError for out_of_range. That's the closest.

Now, decimal_to_binary:

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        if decimal_num >= 0:
            if decimal_num == 0:
                return "0"
            return bin(decimal_num)[2:]
        else:
            # 32-bit two's complement
            val = (1 << 32) + decimal_num
            return format(val, '032b')

But wait: For decimal_num = -2147483648, (1<<32) + (-2147483648) = 2147483648, format '032b' = '10000000000000000000000000000000'. Good.

For decimal_num = -1, (1<<32)-1 = 4294967295, format '032b' = '11111111111111111111111111111111'. Good.

For decimal_num = 0, our code returns "0". Good.

For decimal_num = 1, bin(1)[2:] = "1". Good.

For decimal_num = 255, bin(255)[2:] = "11111111". Good.

Now, what about decimal_num = -0? In Python, -0 is 0. So returns "0". Good.

Now, decimal_to_octal:

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

Does format(-8, 'o') return '-10'? Yes. format(0, 'o') -> '0'. format(8, 'o') -> '10'. Good.

decimal_to_hex:

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

format(-255, 'x') -> '-ff'. format(255, 'x') -> 'ff'. Good.

Now, binary_to_decimal:

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(binary_num, 2)

octal_to_decimal:

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(octal_num, 8)

hex_to_decimal:

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(hex_num, 16)

Now, test with examples:

- decimal_to_binary(5) -> "101"
- binary_to_decimal("101") -> 5
- decimal_to_octal(8) -> "10"
- octal_to_decimal("10") -> 8
- decimal_to_hex(255) -> "ff"
- hex_to_decimal("ff") -> 255

Negative:
- decimal_to_binary(-1) -> "11111111111111111111111111111111"
- binary_to_decimal("11111111111111111111111111111111") -> OverflowError (because 4294967295 > INT_MAX). But wait, is that the expected behavior? Let's double-check C++: std::stoi("11111111111111111111111111111111", nullptr, 2) indeed throws std::out_of_range. Yes. But what if someone expects -1? They might think binary string is two's complement. But the C++ code doesn't do two's complement conversion; it parses as binary number. So our behavior matches.

- decimal_to_octal(-8) -> "-10"
- octal_to_decimal("-10") -> -8
- decimal_to_hex(-255) -> "-ff"
- hex_to_decimal("-ff") -> -255

Now, edge cases:
- binary_to_decimal("") -> ValueError
- binary_to_decimal("  101") -> 5
- binary_to_decimal("101   ") -> 5 (because stops at space)
- binary_to_decimal("101abc") -> 5 (stops at 'a')
- binary_to_decimal("0b101") -> 0? Let's see: parse '0', then 'b' invalid, val=0. C++ std::stoi("0b101", nullptr, 2) returns 0? Yes, because 'b' is not a valid binary digit. Good.

- hex_to_decimal("0x10") -> 0 (parse '0', stop at 'x'). C++ returns 0. Good.
- hex_to_decimal("  -0x10") -> 0. Good.

Now, potential issue: In Python, `format(decimal_num, 'o')` for negative numbers returns '-10'. But C++ std::oct for negative numbers: Does it output "-10" or "37777777770"? Let's verify with actual C++ behavior. I want to be absolutely sure. Let's reason: In C++, `std::cout << std::oct << -8;` I have used it. It prints "-10". Yes, because the sign is not part of the base conversion; the stream inserter for integers prints the sign if negative, then the magnitude in the chosen base. Actually, let's confirm with standard: The `num_put` facet's `do_put` for integers: if the value is negative, it places a minus sign in the output sequence before the digits. The digits are the absolute value converted to the base. So yes.

Now, what about decimal_to_binary for negative? C++ bitset<32> gives two's complement, not sign+magnitude. So our implementation is correct.

Now, one more subtlety: In C++, std::bitset<32>(decimal_num).to_string() for a negative number gives the 32-bit two's complement. But what about for a positive number that has leading zeros? We strip leading zeros. But what if decimal_num is 0? We return "0". Good.

Now, what about decimal_to_binary for a negative number like -2147483648? bitset<32> gives "10000000000000000000000000000000". Our code gives that. Good.

Now, what about decimal_to_binary for a positive number that is exactly 0? We return "0". Good.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "00000000000000000000000000000000"? std::stoi parses as 0. Our parser parses digits "000...0", int = 0, returns 0. Good.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "-0"? std::stoi("-0") returns 0. Our parser: sign -1, digits "0", val=0, returns 0. Good.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "+0"? returns 0.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  -0"? returns 0.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  + 1"? invalid.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1"? returns 1.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1 2"? returns 1.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1\n"? returns 1.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1\n2"? returns 1.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1 2"? returns 1.

Now, what about binary_to_decimal for "  +1_0"? Our parser: valid digits set doesn't include '_', so stops at '_', returns 1. C++ std::stoi("1_0", nullptr, 2) returns 1? Let's see: '1' digit, '_' invalid, so returns 1. Yes. Python int('1_0', 2) would return 2? Actually int('1_0', 2) returns 2? Let's check: underscores are allowed as separators in Python numeric literals, but in int() with base, I think int('1_0', 2) returns 2? It might. But our custom parser avoids that. Good.

Now, what about octal_to_decimal("017") -> std::stoi with base 8 parses "017" as 15? Actually digits "017" -> 0*64 + 1*8 + 7 = 15. Our parser: digits "017", int("017", 8) = 15. Good.

Now, what about octal_to_decimal("08") -> std::stoi parses "0" then stops at '8', returns 0. Our parser: valid digits '0'-'7', parse '0', stop at '8', val=0. Good.

Now, what about octal_to_decimal("0o17") -> std::stoi parses "0" then stops at 'o', returns 0. Our parser: parse '0', stop at 'o', val=0. Good.

Now, what about hex_to_decimal("0x10") -> 0. Good.

Now, what about hex_to_decimal("0X10") -> 0. Good.

Now, what about hex_to_decimal("  -0x10") -> 0.

Now, what about hex_to_decimal("  +0x10") -> 0.

Now, what about hex_to_decimal("  +0x10") -> 0.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def _stoi(s, base):
        # Mimic std::stoi(s, nullptr, base): parse a prefix, allow leading
        # whitespace and an optional sign, then consume valid base digits.
        i = 0
        n = len(s)

        # ASCII whitespace only, matching the C locale used by std::isspace.
        while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
            i += 1

        sign = 1
        if i < n and (s[i] == '+' or s[i] == '-'):
            if s[i] == '-':
                sign = -1
            i += 1

        if base == 2:
            valid = set('01')
        elif base == 8:
            valid = set('01234567')
        elif base == 16:
            valid = set('0123456789abcdefABCDEF')
        else:
            raise ValueError("unsupported base")

        start = i
        while i < n and s[i] in valid:
            i += 1

        if i == start:
            raise ValueError(f"invalid literal for int() with base {base}: {s!r}")

        value = int(s[start:i], base) * sign

        # std::stoi returns int; assume 32-bit signed int range.
        if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
            raise OverflowError("int out of range")

        return value

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        if decimal_num >= 0:
            return "0" if decimal_num == 0 else bin(decimal_num)[2:]
        # 32-bit two's complement, matching std::bitset<32>(decimal_num)
        return format((1 << 32) + decimal_num, '032b')

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(hex_num, 16)
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (py) ===
class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def _stoi(s, base):
        # Mimic std::stoi(s, nullptr, base): parse a prefix, allow leading
        # whitespace and an optional sign, then consume valid base digits.
        i = 0
        n = len(s)

        # ASCII whitespace only, matching the C locale used by std::isspace.
        while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
            i += 1

        sign = 1
        if i < n and (s[i] == '+' or s[i] == '-'):
            if s[i] == '-':
                sign = -1
            i += 1

        if base == 2:
            valid = set('01')
        elif base == 8:
            valid = set('01234567')
        elif base == 16:
            valid = set('0123456789abcdefABCDEF')
        else:
            raise ValueError("unsupported base")

        start = i
        while i < n and s[i] in valid:
            i += 1

        if i == start:
            raise ValueError(f"invalid literal for int() with base {base}: {s!r}")

        value = int(s[start:i], base) * sign

        # std::stoi returns int; assume 32-bit signed int range.
        if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
            raise OverflowError("int out of range")

        return value

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        if decimal_num >= 0:
            return "0" if decimal_num == 0 else bin(decimal_num)[2:]
        # 32-bit two's complement, matching std::bitset<32>(decimal_num)
        return format((1 << 32) + decimal_num, '032b')

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return NumberConverter._stoi(hex_num, 16)
